ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e225	Housing policy is high on the public agenda and is subject to significant interest. To this end, the Committee held a hearing on the matter on 30 April, and the hearing was attended by a wide range of organisations, which used the opportunity to make their views known in the notification. The key challenge today is that there has been an imbalance between supply and demand over time. The supply of new dwellings has not kept up with demand. This has contributed to an increase in the price of dwellings which have far exceeded the growth in construction costs and which is higher than the wage growth in society. The Committee agrees that the dwellings being built today will remain for many years to come. Therefore, it is so essential that they support important societal considerations. The Committee has underlined in its comments that it is a political responsibility to ensure that the qualities not sufficiently taken into account by market players are respected. The key instrument to ensure these objectives is planning under the Planning and Building Act. The use of objections is central when we discuss spatial planning in the municipalities. There has been a topic that has been raised by a large number of municipalities with the Committee, including: on the many trips we've had all over the country. A Joint Committee has taken note of the report entitled ‘Impact of the Anti-Fraud Office on Local Self-Government’, prepared by Aplan Viak on behalf of the KS. It appears that objections do not always work in line with the provisions of the law, inter alia. as regards the provisions on early participation in the planning processes of all relevant opposition bodies, including both state, county and local authorities. The Committee considers that the provisions on the waiver of the right of objection should be exploited more actively by municipalities. In its comments, the Committee stressed that the quality of the dwelling extends beyond the construction technical solution. The concept of quality also includes aspects such as plan solutions, liberality, material use, energy use, outdoor areas and accessibility. The quality of the dwelling is also affected by the environment. Therefore, housing needs to be seen in the context of site development and transport solutions. In the Committee's view, it is a political responsibility to facilitate sustainable solutions to address these concerns. There is also a broad majority in the Committee, all with the exception of the members of the Progress Party, which shows that housing policy has been largely successful. Nearly eight out of ten households own their own dwelling, we have a high standard of housing, and we have liberal dwellings. In Norway, fortunately, few people are completely outside the housing market. For this broad majority, a good housing is together with the possibility of work, the right to education and the need for health and care services, the pillars of the Norwegian welfare community. It is the individual who has a responsibility to obtain a dwelling. At the same time, it is a public responsibility to facilitate a well-functioning market, so that everyone has the opportunity to obtain a dwelling and can live well. The majority indicate that it is central to facilitating the construction of enough dwellings. It is the task of the state to ensure good framework conditions for housing. Municipalities have the task of securing and regulating sufficient space for residential purposes, providing rapid and efficient building management and providing municipal rental housing. In summary, the Committee agrees on some key premises and challenges, but we strongly disagree on the means of achieving them. I assume that the representatives of the respective parties present their specific points of view and proposals. I am pleased that we have received a big message that addresses the broad housing policy, including the housing supply. For the Workers' Party, it is a central political task to facilitate the construction of adequate housing. Here there is a distinction between the Right and the Advance Party on one side and the Working Party on the other. Norway currently has one of the least regulated housing markets in Europe. It is a paradox that in a country where there is a great deal else of public responsibility, we do not have a clearer political position of responsibility for securing people somewhere to live. For the Workers' Party, housing is just one of the four pillars of welfare policy, together with education, health and labour policy. Together, these are the basis for everyone to have the opportunity to create a good life. It is a state responsibility to ensure good framework conditions for housing construction. The municipalities have a task, and the state has a task. We want municipalities with offensive and forward-looking housing policies that meet a constructive and coordinated state. In summary, we want to take housing policy in a slightly different direction. We want a little bit less market and a little bit more policy. The dwellings that are being built now will be the dwellings that we use for decades to come. Therefore, today's housing policy must be forward-looking, even in terms of quality of what is being built. We live longer and longer, and most of us want to stay as far as possible in our own home when we grow old. The design of dwellings must take this into account. If we are to increase the proportion of available dwellings, we must make demands on the availability of the dwellings that are being built today. Despite the fact that some construction operators claim otherwise, several reports show that it is not very much more expensive to build dwellings with good availability. There are also several actors in the industry who demonstrate that well-accessible dwellings can be built with marginal additional costs. The assumption is that good planning and design takes into account availability from the outset. For the Workers' Party, it is little future-oriented to take a step back and in 2013 start building dwellings that are not accessible to everyone. The overall aim is to reduce energy use in buildings. We discussed this issue, among other things. thorough in relation to the processing of the building message earlier. Then we have to build buildings that are energy efficient. Then it is a little strange to note that the largest opposition party, the Progress Party, will return to the energy requirements of 1997 – i.e. more than 15 years old standards for energy efficiency. It is possible, as it was said from among others. The representative Amundsen in the debate we had yesterday, that the Progress Party recognises that there are man-made climate change and that the Progress Party supported some of the proposals that were being discussed yesterday. But this is a concrete measure in which the Progress Party wants to reverse changes, which is not a constructive contribution to meeting the challenge we are facing. For the Workers' Party, it is a goal that it should be as simple as possible to build dwellings. At the same time, we are concerned that what is being built should be built with good quality and with the least possible error. Therefore, there has to be a balance between simplification measures and quality requirements. The government announced simplifications in the planning and construction processes in the building message, and we will follow up on this with concrete simplification measures. One of the measures that will reduce the amount of proceedings in municipalities is the removal of the obligation to apply for simple measures on own property, to mention one example. The opposition will criticise us for the lack of action and for the absence of action in the notification. We do not agree with that. Of course, we can have a discussion whether there is the amount of action, or whether it is the quality of action that should be important. Firstly, the Communication contains a wide range of measures, and it sets out a whole range of important actions to be taken to achieve the objectives. But let me mention some of the most important steps for the Workers' Party. First of all, it's about student housing. This government has had a high rate of expansion in student housing, and we're going to continue that. During the last year of the Bondevik II government, 314 new student homes were built. In its period, the government of Stoltenberg has built about 1,000 new dwellings a year, and we are now increasing the rate. For the current year, 1 500 new student homes have been promised. For the Labour Party, we have even greater ambitions, because student housing is an important contribution to ease the pressure on the housing market in press areas. We have said that we want to step up to about 3,000 built dwellings a year. In addition, there is a desire to do something about the cost framework for student housing and to increase the rate of grant to about 50 pst. Of course, this is the case with this measure, as with all other measures: one thing is what you say and write, but it has to be followed up in annual budgetary decisions. It's our ambition to do that. The house bank has been, is and will be one of the most important tools we have in housing policy. It is the government’s goal that everyone should live safely. Extending the lending framework, as we have done on several occasions, is therefore one of several contributions to increased construction activity and improved quality of living in Norway. This will also be done in the context of the review of the revised national budget tomorrow — an increase in the framework of 20 billion. DKK to 25 billion kr for 2013. In conclusion, this down-to-earth notification addresses the broad housing policy, including the housing supply, which I was at the beginning. But the social housing work is also a high priority, it has had a high priority throughout the period and will have it in the future. Housing and services need to be increasingly seen in the context. In order to succeed in the social work of housing, the country needs to find the context. It's not always as easy, nor can it be the responsibility of municipalities to see all these contexts. A coordinated country provides better municipal solutions for individuals. That is why we are making, and alerts, a new strategy that brings together and targets public efforts. It is still an objective that everyone should be able to live well in Norway, regardless of the size of the wallet.

As the case spokesman was also in the case, Norway is a country where most people own their own housing. There has been broad political agreement on this, and it has also been part of the Norwegian lightning net, traditions build in a way that one should own his own dwelling – especially in the district of Norway, but also in the cities. About 80 pst. own his own dwelling, and approx. 20 pst. the lessee. It is also true that, in recent years, developments have moved in the direction that a person not only owns one dwelling during his life. One buys a dwelling and sells, then buys a new one and sells, etc. A change in the size, quality and location of the apartment depends on the private family situation. The last 20 years have seen a strong price rise in housing. Of course, it has been quite unproblematic for those who initially own a dwelling, those who are in the housing market. However, this sharp rise in prices has caused many young people to have problems establishing themselves in the housing market. Both housing prices and rental prices have risen, but housing prices have increased most. It has also been stronger in housing prices than in construction costs and the overall rise in prices in the market. The reason we've had this increase – which other countries haven't had, other countries have gone the other way – is that we've had a good development in the economy, and we've had a high oil price. There's something that obviously doesn't come from the government's work, but a high oil price has led to a very high level of activity in the oil sector, and it has had a good impact on the economy. One has also received good revenue to the public sector, which has allowed one to increase the number of public input. As a result, the demand for housing has gone up. The financial crisis has given low interest rates. This, of course, has resulted in a better purchasing power for the individual and has been able to increase its debt without any appreciable increase in the interest burden. The financial crisis has also resulted in a large immigration into Norway. Last year we had a net increase of 40,000 in the number of job seekers and 10,000 in the number of asylum seekers to Norway. They also need to buy – they are asked for housing, which means that prices are pushed up. The last point, which comes from the government's policy, is that there are several detailed rules. The number of countersigns has increased when it comes to regulating to land ready for construction. This means that regulation takes a very long time, and one thing is not to meet it until after many years. As the case spokesperson was concerned, there are also new requirements for the construction of the dwellings, for example. energy efficiency – a requirement that has come and that both costs and causes fewer dwellings to be built. The same applies to wheelchair availability – regardless of the size of the dwelling. This has meant that the smallest apartments are not built at all. The result is that the smallest apartments, down to 30 m2, rise most in the market – due to the fact that no more of them are built. It's because it's almost impossible to adjust these to wheelchairs. The party of progress is concerned about rising housing prices. It is a fact that many young people have problems getting into the housing market and that these developments will worsen when additional requirements for energy efficiency are introduced. This will make it worse because it will be built fewer and more expensive dwellings. If prices are pushed further up, the potential for fall will be even greater. We mean that the state should not go in and regulate in detail how to build a dwelling up in Finnmark, in the West, in Hedmark, etc. We believe that it should mainly be up to the individual builder and purchaser to decide which standards and qualities to set for each building. We nevertheless believe that there must be certain minimum requirements, for example. the energy requirements and the minimum standard introduced in 1997. We see no need for politicians to make any additional demands beyond that. We don't know the best for others, we think people know the best for themselves. Norway has a comprehensive regulatory framework and legislation that one has to comply with when building dwellings. One has these laws to comply with when building buildings: planning and building laws with regulations and guidelines – the building part – planning and building laws – the plan part – cultural monuments, energy legislation, fire and explosion protection laws, electrical supervision laws, housing laws, work environment laws, discrimination laws, consumer laws, housing registration laws, craft services laws, property brokerage laws, disposal laws, tax laws, etc., contract and contract law – public procurement law, contract law, purchasing law – and environmental law. In addition, what we've been up to in the past is public hearing bodies in connection with construction projects. So we have 22 agencies that can come up with contradictions that can stop and prevent building construction, so we don't have any national agencies that are working to promote building construction. The Progress Party therefore believes that this message underlines in a good way that the problem in Norway is not too little housing policy, but too much and bad housing policy. One simply has too many rules and too much detail control, which makes it too slow to cover the increased demand for housing that we have seen in recent years. That's the point: the public has to regulate less, no more, if the housing market is to balance. We mean that something is ripping faults when it goes five, six, seven years just to regulate to a building plot empty. Of course, there is a lack of land in many places, and thus an explosive rise in housing prices, especially in central areas. The party of progress therefore believes that we must be very reluctant to impose detailed requirements on new buildings, and that developments there must be natural, without pressure from central government powers. What, then, should be done to achieve a better balance in the housing market? Well, there's a couple of points that the Progress Party is occupied with, for example. red tape and detailed management needs to be reduced. There are too many countersigns, too much detail control. The last thing that came in was independent control of dwellings, which makes it more expensive. This has to go down to a much lower level. It has to go down to a minimum. This also applies to the contradictions – they have to be limited. Many good construction projects have been prevented because of contradictions. Now, of course, I am aware of the government's efforts to coordinate these contradictions, that the County will take care of it. I don't think there's any solution at all. I just think that it can give these contradictions even greater weight and strength, making it harder to build ahead. The energy efficiency requirement must be reversed. I think if you're busy saving energy, if you're busy increasing the energy surplus in Norway, this can be done in completely different ways, with positive tools: support schemes for, for example. one-stroke pump and by providing for more expansion of hydropower, etc. That would be better. That there should be passive house standards on all buildings, wherever they are built – in Finnmark, in the West, in Hedmark, Oslo, etc. – would be very wrong. The requirement for wheelchair access must also be removed, and it applies particularly to the smallest types of occupants. It goes without saying that there will be the smallest dwellings that become the most heavily affected by this requirement, which will have the biggest problems and the biggest costs of adaptation. The capital requirement of 15 pst. must go away. Banks must have a banking assessment of who is to be loaned and not. If you've been studying for many years and want to buy a dwelling, you may not have that much of your capital, but you may have a very good income that makes it no problem to borrow a mortgage, even if you borrow 100 pst. So it has to be up to the banks to assess what is a banker's defence to give in loans. The BSU system must be improved. This means that those who are mobile and move and change the dwelling more often have to pay a higher cost in document fee than the other must. The document fee is a fiscal fee. The typing fee covers the costs that the State has to bear in order to light things up. The document fee is then an additional cost. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e350	How we live is largely based on how we individuals experience our social situation. It is quite interesting for us who have been involved in housing policy for some years, that this Communication does not contain a programme statement or overall vision for housing policy in Norway. Sure, it contains a lot of nice words about how nice it is to live safely, but I guess we didn't need a big message to be remembered. In the past, housing policy operated with a national target of how much of the average disposable income to cover the costs of living. If I were to formulate a goal for housing policy, it would become something like this: the goal of housing policy is to facilitate that everyone can obtain a decent and safe housing that can be financed through ordinary income, by enabling the housing market to provide a variety of types of housing that suit the individual household and living conditions, and that public housing solutions and housing subsidies should be more targeted to help disadvantaged groups better. One of the features of this Communication is that the State has not fully clarified its governance ambitions. I think it is very important that the state directs its governance ambitions towards what policy is for governing, namely coordinated decisions relating to the transport of roads, of railways, of land planning at large. Then you can play on a market-driven housing production that is responsible for the parties that participate in it, and actually also with a slightly greater consumer influence by making their own preferences about the qualities they want in their housing. An example of the fact that the country is involved in many subordinate and detailed ideas about how Norway is to be expanded is, of course, the widespread use of objections. I refer here to the local authority's organisation, KS, which has had a broad survey that showed that there are hardly any major spatial plans that have not been met by state objections. Therefore, government agencies need to reduce the use of objections and they need to be coordinated and limited thematically. The objectors must be reminded of the reason for holding the objector. So it's only to be used if it's of national importance or of strong regional interest. The right considers that, in order to achieve a target of lower housing prices, it is essential that the construction industry can cut its costs. The construction industry must also contribute itself to more rational production. Today, for example, 22,000 dwellings are built on pre-regulated plots only in Oslo. Nevertheless, many builders are detained because state policy contains ambiguous signals, which give them uncertainty. Construction has a desire to build more dwellings faster. Daily, however, the industry faces challenges that stand in the way of the needs of the industry. The everyday life of enterprises — as reported to us through consultations and other — is characterised by the existence of a through-bureaucratic field dominated by forms, the application requirements of a number of non-cooperative public bodies. This applies to both small and large-scale projects and, in particular, to large and important projects in and around cities. This message comes entirely at the brink of a boom – and at the very end of the eight-year period that the red-greens have run the country – and we must note that this government has not been particularly hasty about producing this message, which unfortunately also fits into the general impression that one has not been pursuing an active housing policy. There is also a significant gap between what is achieved in the form of housing production and what is reported as the need for housing. It is, of course, nice that we have finally received a big message about housing policy, but it is therefore too late. In addition, the communication is very unclear about what you want, and to a large extent these measures consist in implementing current policies. Almost no concrete measures are announced to address the housing issue. I want to reflect a little – as the representative Hagesetter did – on the form of the message. There have been many representative initiatives here in the Chamber that have just pushed the call for housing. So I have to be almost so bold as to say that part of the messages has the clear purpose of not notifying about where to take new initiatives, but rather to calm down and say that basically everything is hurt well and we are not going to do anything special about it. It reminds me a little of conversational therapy – if you don’t get to do anything with the underlying problems, it’s often nice to talk about them at least. I must also say that under this government we also have others who pursue housing policy. Financial supervision is managed by a person who personally believes that we are now in a housing bubble, and who in public writing has drawn the parallel to Ireland, a country characterised by a high degree of labour migration. When economic activity turned back in Ireland, many naturally moved back and left a hole in the housing market, which led to pressure on prices. This is the same person as – with a stroke – increased the equity requirement by 50 p.m., in December 2011, without – as far as I understand it – the government was informed about this, or at least it had the respect for the expert council that she was involved in this. So bureaucracy controls Norway, and they do it in a way that has an impact on housing — while the government does not control this. The specific equity requirement is a good example. To address the demand for some of the markets, the instrument was targeted at those who were first-time start-ups. For equity, there is no problem for those who are going to buy their second dwelling, because they have free values in the old when the market is going largely up. So this was simply a'mass destruction weapon' calculated precisely on the first-time starters, from which we already know the oldest demand, the cheapest and the smallest dwellings in the market, and which is therefore not the group that pushes prices up. This has been almost impossible to have a dialogue with the Council of State on, but I will come back to this later in the debate. So it's not just to say that you're responsible for housing issues – you have to actually also control the parameters that seem much stronger on the housing demand than on the staging messages, which we spend time here in Stortinget. It reminds me of another good proposal that we have made in the notes and in the proposals, namely that we want the government's responsibility to be clarified by putting the planning and housing issues in one department, so we do not have the liability transport that we have seen here on some occasions when we have asked about coherent housing policies. The right has noted with some concern the use of language in the communication on how the further taxation of dwellings and other real estate becomes. I think we're just going to stress here today that housing investment is one of the most important things we do in our lives, and that it's very important for people's security that these framework conditions are fixed, so you can trust them over the years, and that you don't cut the tax on housing, that our homes don't become a nice treasure. In 2005, there were 76 pst. of all children aged 1 to 5 years in kindergarten. Now 9 out of 10 children in kindergarten. This will have major consequences for municipalities with low tax revenues. During this four-year period, the red-green government has further redistributed tax revenues so that tax-weak municipalities are transferred more from tax-intensive municipalities. The reason for this is to balance the differences between municipalities. Here, the Working Party and the Red-Green Government are completely different from the Right and Advance Party. We want smaller differences between municipalities, not larger. The balancing of tax revenues is a choice based on ideology and a desire for the direction in which the society of Norway will evolve. Some medium-sized and large municipalities will lose million amounts, which will obviously affect the well-being of these municipalities, regardless of whether the change occurs within one, three or five years. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e1098	Unfortunately, the relationship between the State and the municipality is characterised by some mutual mistrust. The policy of today's government is that municipalities receive grants for the first year a measure is introduced, but then steps down or remove the grant. At the same time, we see further challenges in the implementation of the Cooperation Reform and its financing. During today's government, municipalities and county councils have been given more and more statutory tasks without funding. This means that municipalities must either cut off their other responsibilities or, if they are not able to do so, take up loans. It is true that the local government sector has been subject to a significant build-up of debt under this government, which is quite alarming, and KS has calculated the debt, if corrective measures are not taken and the price is corrected, to make 75 pst. annual operational expenditure. The need for freedom for Kommune-Norge must also mean freedom from underfunded reforms. In fact, it is the government that is the greatest threat to the freedom of municipalities through this lack of funding and promises on behalf of local politicians that puts them in an impossible situation of expectations, because they are unable to deliver. I think we can establish that the freedom to prioritise in line with local policy needs has been systematically weakened. However, the most important freedom for municipalities is the freedom to provide good enough services and freedom for local adaptation. The main threat to this freedom is that there are no resources to develop and maintain skills-based, stable, locally adapted services in the areas considered to be the most central ones: school, health and care. Of course, a good deal can be achieved through a community reform. The cost of administrative staff can be reduced if you have a slightly larger population to allocate administrative costs to. But those who believe that organisational changes will be enough to restore the lack of trust in Kommune-Norway will probably be too optimistic. It is also a problem that municipal autonomy is legally weak. What I'm thinking about is that in many contexts, municipalities and states see differences in both the method of calculation and legal conflicts. Today, conflict resolution works so that the state decides as it pleases, usually after extensive use of time. We see the need to establish an independent dispute settlement body between the State and the municipality. In fact, it was also foreseen when Norway ratified the European Council Convention on Local Self-Government in 1989. So we have a universal obligation to give municipalities the means to try their case in a discussion or conflict with state agencies of an unhidden body. We had also looked forward to the establishment of such an independent dispute resolution body, and I refer to the proposal for a representative, Document 8:32 for 2011–2012, to introduce such a dispute resolution body. To sum up, the right wants more power for municipal elected, less power for state governments. When the municipalities were deprived of income from corporation tax, they lost an important incentive for local economic development and value creation. We want the municipalities to retain a greater proportion of their own income through corporate tax, as it will trigger interest in facilitating local business. The red-green option is to treat municipalities more and more as welfare-state clients, and then it's no wonder they want to take that role. They will then spend more of their time influencing state budgets and convincing them of their lack of opportunities, rather than seeing the opportunities that are local to stimulate business and bring growth in individual municipalities. Therefore, the Right wants a comprehensive review of the income system of the local government sector, changes to ensure and stimulate positive developments in the income of municipalities. The aim is to build up under local self-government by retaining a share of corporation tax and a greater share of own tax revenue. This should, of course, take account of the income guarantee scheme, which guarantees that municipalities will always have a time horizon to adapt to new regimes and new income rules.

In 2013, the municipality was asked for 55 people, and 55 people were adopted to live. 79 pst. are in work or education after completion of the introduction programme in 2012. In 2012, they were asked to live in 15 people, and 15 people lived in them. Corresponding in 2013: They were requested to settle for 25, and receive 25. They have a positive attitude to keep doing that. We could move on to other municipalities, but I don't have time for that. I think this illustrates an important point. This is also about the will and ability of the municipalities, and not just about money from the state.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2186	In this case, the Progress Party asks the Government to ensure that foreign citizens employed in the public sector have sufficient Norwegian skills to ensure good quality and to prevent misunderstandings in the meeting between the users of public services and public sector employees. The Committee also asked for the Minister’s opinion in this matter. She now replied, as in the previous round, that it is the individual undertaking itself that is responsible for recruiting qualified staff for vacancies and assessing the requirements to be imposed, including: to Norwegian knowledge. At the same time, she points out that after 1 September of this year mandatory tests will be carried out for those with a right and/or obligation to participate in training in Norwegian and social sciences. Such documentation will be useful for employers to recruit new employees. In addition, language training will be one of several actions in the forthcoming action plan for better use of the skills of immigrants in the labour market. The introduction of language requirements for certain health professions shall be considered. Money is allocated to the current budget for language training for health professionals. An instructor for the addition of health professionals has been developed, highlighting the importance of language skills. The Committee shares the view of the proposers that the management of Norwegian is important in order to ensure good quality in the public service, but the other parties agree with the Council of State that this must be the responsibility of individual employers, whether national or municipal. The majority, which consists of all the other parties, therefore argues that the proposal for this to be a State overall responsibility is rejected. There have been long-standing media-related situations in which users of public services are met with non-Norwegian-speaking employees. We have examples of fixed doctors who are unable to communicate, who are unable to understand what the patient is saying, and who are unable to communicate to the patient what might happen. We have several examples of this. Then we have some examples of kindergarten employees. There have been complaints from parents who are unable to communicate with the employees in the kindergarten where their children go. They are unable to communicate what is to be said about the child who may be special, nor are they receiving feedback from the employees, because the employees are unable to communicate. We have examples of mismedication, and we have examples of communication that has gone so wrong that there has actually been a risk of life. When we have such situations, and we are not willing to see if we should perhaps have a national norm, a national target, namely that those who are going to work in the public sector and communicate with users should be able to understand Norwegian, then we have a problem in Norway. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2380	The Presidency sees it as very important that legislative proposals made by the representatives of wholesalers be reviewed and quality assured by competent professional bodies, both in legal matters, in relation to the relationship with the Basic Law and in relation to other legal rules. A clear majority of the presidency believes that there are good opportunities for such assistance also with today's organisation of the legislative work. All legislative proposals – both those made by the government in a proposal and those made by the representatives – are reviewed and proofread by Stortingen's administration and quality assurance also in the case of legal matters. Representatives also have the opportunity to address the Stortingen administration for legal and legal assistance with the design of legislative proposals prior to their submission. This plan is only used temporarily. Representatives also have the opportunity to ask the Administration of the Grand Duchy to address legal and other professional issues related to legal work. For example, the Investigation Section may: provide background information and evaluate the legal consequences of a legislative proposal. However, the investigation section has limited capacity. This has a resource side and needs to be seen in the context of the fact that legislative activity in the Storting may vary considerably throughout the year and from year to year. I might want to put the greatest emphasis on the last one. To put it another way, and more directly, the activity tends to be associated with an upcoming choice. Therefore, of course, capacity is not always adapted to the activity of Stortinget. In the view of the majority, the need to strengthen administrative investigation capacity must therefore be assessed on a regular basis in the context of the examination of the Storting’s own budget. The majority of the Presidency believes that it is not desirable to build an investigative apparatus in the Administration of Storting as a full alternative to the government at its disposal. Let me say it this way: it will be a pretty substantial manpower task. In Norwegian constitutional practice, the government is making the vast majority of legislative proposals. The government has therefore built up a comprehensive investigation device for, among other things. to be able to fulfil the obligation to provide information to Stortinget under the Constitution § 82. The legal quality assurance of legislative proposals has also been added to the government in our constitutional practice, at least since the creation of a separate law office, as it was then called, in the Ministry of Justice in 1885. This office, later the Law Department, should have a primary responsibility for the legal and technical aspects of the legal work. Although the Department of Law was organised as a unit in a department, formally subject to the governing and constitutional responsibility of the Council of State, it has been argued that the Department provides its legal professional judgements independently of the Department’s political direction. The opinion of the Legal Service as a professionally independent body in legislative matters is also expressed by the request of the Stortinget or a Grand Committee for legal assistance. It is rare that requests for such assistance, to a reasonable extent, are not complied with. In accordance with established practice, the Committees also have the opportunity to ask the Technical Council a written question on a matter for consideration by the Committee, including requests for legal assistance. The Presidency of the Grand Duchy has pointed out on several occasions that it is of great importance for the Grand Duchy's legislative activities to comply with such requests by the Committees and the Party Groups for legal assistance. However, understanding must also be shown that departments cannot meet such requests if a very large amount of work is required, and – I would like to add – if it requires policy assessments beyond what a law department can naturally do. Following the majority's assessment, there are overall good opportunities for legal and regulatory assistance in the preparation of legislative proposals in the party groups or committees, either in the administration of the Stortinge or by submitting a request to the government. The majority of the Presidency therefore sees little reason to strengthen the investigation capacity of the Storting on a permanent basis with a view to legal assistance. The Presidency will propose that the proposal for a representative should therefore not be accepted.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2397	Let me start by referring to the comments and the proposal in the vote. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2422	Formal power in Norway is divided into three: between magnificence, government and courts. The assumption for such a third is that there is a certain degree of autonomy in each of these institutions. That means that the courts will take their decisions on the laws that are made, without interference from the politicians in the process. That means that the government is the one who is preparing and presenting matters to the Storting. And that means that the Storting is the sovereign to consider the issues that the government is presenting, and to adopt laws. There are no watertight bulkheads between these three arrows. It's e.g. the government which points out judges of the Supreme Court, which shall judge by the laws adopted by the Grand Duchy. And, above all, the Storing itself can take the initiative to design and adopt laws, regardless of the government, either through representative proposals or through the consideration of government matters. In both cases, the Storting is free to adopt exactly the laws that the Storing believes the country needs, regardless of the government. But this autonomy requires a legislative competence. That legal competence does not have the Storting as an institution today. Today, only the government has such competence, in the Department of Law of the Ministry of Justice. Of course, the parties on the Storting may acquire such a legal preparatory competence themselves by buying such legal services privately or even hiring the best lawyers. But such services are usually expensive, and it will allow those parties with best advice to buy the best services, while smaller parties will be cut off from this possibility. This will not be a successful system because the economy of the groups should not play a role in the ability of large-scale representatives or the party groups to carry out their act as lawmakers. In my opinion, this has to do with fundamental principles of democracy. Nor should such a role play the question of whether a party is in government. Position and opposition should have equal opportunities to investigate their legislative proposals. Today, the system operates in such a way that it is the government parties that govern the Ministry of Justice, and thus the priorities of the Department of Law. I remember that a better balance between the rights of the parties in the opposition and the position was also the reason why SV took the initiative in its time to set up the Swedish Investigation Department. It was just created to ensure that opposition parties would not be dependent on the government and the ministries to investigate issues that they would have investigated. It works in our opinion well according to purpose. Therefore, SV supports such an investigation of its own legislative competence in Stortinget, as discussed in the representative’s proposal. The reason why SV has a separate proposal in the case is that we believe that such a legal competence must be to give the local representatives quick and good help in assessing their own proposals for law and getting help to formulate their own alternative legal text for the government's proposal, regardless of the Ministry of Justice's law department, not to check quality or to ensure the quality of the work carried out by the Legal Service, as the Progress Party is calling for. Should this be desirable, I must remind you that the Stortinget already has the opportunity to avail itself of the constitutional court Stortinget actually has legal assistance or to have legal proposals checked up against the Constitution of the Supreme Court. I shall deal with my proposal on the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2557	In the great story of modern Norway, an ever better public health belongs at home. In the 1900s, the average life expectancy increased by 30 years, and it continues to increase. The Norwegian population is in good health. Both my grandparents had the sibling who died of tuberculosis. Public health has stagnated the infectious diseases over the last 100 years. Toothpuss and fluorine have given better teeth, we have access to prevention and vaccines, and most houses are connected to waterworks that provide clean water. Most people have good health and live long. Sickness and death are increasingly caused by the way we live: food with too much salt, sugar and fat as well as too little physical activity. In 2011, a UN summit concluded that non-infectious diseases constitute one of the greatest threats to human development this century. We're not going to solve these problems at the doctor's office or at the operating table. The Communication describes the challenges and makes proposals for action. The recipient is not primarily healthcare, but the whole community. The principle of ‘health in all we do’ implies that the health of the population must be taken into account in all policies and across society. The Communication draws attention to health and quality of life factors rather than to a disease perspective. I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation. We don't agree on anything, but there's a lot we're dealing with. The entire Committee shares the government's ambition for broad mobilisation for public health. While Norway is a society of small differences in many other areas, there are systematic health differences between income and education groups. Norwegian higher education men live 6.3 years longer than men without. A fair society, without great differences between people, provides better conditions for good public health work. People's health is a result of how we set up society. The communication highlights the importance of volunteering in public health work. Freedom and public health are two sides of the same coin. It becomes more evident where non-governmental organisations have a direct responsibility for health promotion activities, as we see in sport and speed. Organisations that work to raise awareness and provide an offer to persons with specific disabilities or diagnoses play another important role. The National Public Health Association does both. Indigenous schools and offers to people with dementia are important. The same is true of the organisation’s efforts to influence society’s attitudes and knowledge of dementia. We also have non-governmental organisations that work with prevention as a whole. The organisation MOT has good results in prevention among young people, long before problems occur. It's an important job. In addition, all voluntary work is health promotion, wherever it happens. Cultural activities, tour groups, teams and associations are – no matter what they do – good venues for social association, knowledge and shared experiences. The Communication also invites business operators to cooperate. In a few years, Norwegian imports of palm oil fell sharply: from 26 000 tonnes in 2005 to 5000 tonnes in 2012 — during a period when consumption in the rest of the world has doubled. This is due to good cooperation between industry and the authorities. And while the committee examined the notification, the news came that similar cooperation was established for the marketing of children. That nutritionists in this way take social responsibility is great. There is consensus on a lot in the field of public health, but there are differences in the view of the balance between individual and community responsibilities. The working party is concerned that health is not a moral issue. Everyone is responsible for their own health, but the community is responsible for everyone's health. Per Fugelli has warned as follows: ‘The individualisation of preventive health work has moral side effects. [[...]] [[...]] Those who smoke, eat chips, and sit on the sofa instead of throwing themselves into the ake tray with red eplexin, are weak, stupid and guilty. People don't get any more life against such stamping." [[...]] Life’s face and life’s joy are important. In the hearings, the government’s goal of one hour of daily physical activity in the school was widely endorsed. Physical activity is important for the growth and development of children. Trudvang school in Sogndal has had 60 minutes of daily mobility for all students for two years. Students participating have improved in grades compared to the national average on national tests in grades 8 and 9. More attention needs to be paid to the mental health of children and young people. Studies show that children and young people experience healthcare as unavailable. There are knowledge-based methods of prevention and early intervention for children with symptoms of mental health disorders, such as: Psychological First Aid. It is important that such tools are used, for example: in the school health service. Strengthening school health is really great, and it's hugely important. The youth of the Red Cross has pointed out that the quality of life and sex education varies too much. That young people can develop good and healthy attitudes to themselves, their sexuality and health as well as increased respect for the borders and integrity of others is important for public health. Changing eating habits is more than knowing the different nutrients. The Geitmyra food culture centre for children is making an important effort to spread matted and agricultural understanding. The Centre is financed by public and private funds and is an important player for kindergartens, schools and volunteers in Oslo. The Centre is also a resource for other parts of the country. We are concerned that they will also have a framework that will enable them to play such a role in the future. Demos represents a major challenge both for public health and for care services. Research shows that different measures can slow disease development and improve patient and related quality of life. The Committee is committed to seeing the link between life and goals of preventing and slowing down the development of dementia. In the notes about this, there is an error in the setting. It is – among other things – also a good public health measure. Environment means a lot to quality of life and health. Driving good urban and urban development is also about promoting public health. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2708	Today, when the Storting deals with a new public health report, it is a good tradition that every ten years a global report on this issue is being addressed. The size treated St.meld. No 37 for 1992-1993, Health promotion and prevention challenges, 1993. In 2003 Stortinget St.meld. No 16 for 2002–2003, prescription for a healthier Norway. It contained strategies for the ten-year period we have now left behind. Today, we are addressing a growing communication on strategies for the next period, under the heading ‘Good health — shared responsibility’. If we look at the content and objectives of these two messages – one presented by a centre – the Right-Registration and the present, presented by a red-green government – we find that there are not the differences that appear to be the most obvious, but the similarities. The description of the situation in the main message Recipe for a healthier Norway was that trends in society pose new health challenges. Examples were increased globalisation, a multicultural society, a demanding working life and drug problems. One pointed out that the World Health Organisation pointed out that one third of the overall burden of disease in industrialized countries is due to the five risk factors of tobacco, alcohol, high blood pressure, cholesterol and obesity. The objectives of the Annual Report Recipe for a healthier Norway were to have a policy that could contribute to: several years of life with good health in the population as a whole to reduce health inequalities between social, ethnic and gender groups. So have we succeeded in that ten-year period? Both yes and no. We have succeeded in getting more years of life in the population as a whole. However, a key finding from the 2010 global disease burden study shows that increasing life expectancy is associated with several years of reduced health and disease burden from non-lethal conditions. While life expectancy has increased for all, groups of long-term education and high incomes have had the best development. The results of a recent Norwegian register study with data up to 2009 suggest that social health differences have increased over time. Other signs are more positive. In the case of obesity and obesity in children and young people, we see from the latest figures that the proportion is falling for both girls and boys. Many suggest that the onset age of alcohol is increasing, and that the proportion of illicit drug users is falling among the youngest. But the big picture is that life expectancy is rising as a result of our success in creating a welfare society. Better living conditions, safer jobs, regulated working time, the economy to buy rich food and education for everyone is the main explanation. The fact that we are getting older in Norway is therefore the main expression that we have succeeded in many areas. At the same time, we see that lifestyle diseases form a major part of the disease picture and contribute to the decline in the quality of the ‘bad’ life years that we have won. This means that a significant proportion of patients treated in our hospitals in the coming years will be related to lifestyle diseases. Some suggest that as much as 80 pst. the treatment will be linked to this. These are diseases that we could have largely prevented by better success in other areas of society. To put it simply, in the years to come, our hospital expenses will increasingly be the price that we society will have to pay for failing in other areas of society. The main solution for public health is therefore in policy areas other than direct health policy. We see that the main difference in health is related to education and labour relations. The school is therefore very central — not primarily to compensate for the failure of parents or society to follow up their children, but to do what is precisely the school’s primary task: education. What will mean most for public health is that we can create a school with opportunities for everyone. What does international research tell us about what's most important to achieve that goal? Is it school food, is there more physical activity in school, is it more elective? No – the answer is quite simple: it's the teacher. Five years with a competent teacher are enough to balance the differences between the pupils due to the level of education and income of their parents. Therefore, the Right's clear priority is that a teacher's vow is the next big initiative, where the State has to take responsibility for the school. This is one of the most important things we can do for public health. It implies the right and obligation of continuing education for teachers who are in school today. It involves the introduction of a five-year master's education for teachers who will start school in the future, and it involves not least the possibility of careers in classrooms, but not outside the classroom. That's where we live and in what the social physician Per Birdeli calls "our team," the good life is lived and created. If one is to have room to develop the good communities, families and networks, these need to be strengthened. In English there is the word «empowerment», but I have not been able to find an equally comprehensive Norwegian word for this. Strengthening local democracy, strengthening the family and strengthening and drying to delegate power locally are therefore an essential part of public health work. In the right we say that the spread of power makes society more robust. In this context, volunteering has a dual function. Studies show that a key explanation of the great trust we have in each other and in our social institutions in the Nordic countries is that a large proportion of the population is engaged in voluntary organisations. The security that this trust provides is essential for our public health – not least mental public health. At the same time, the voluntary organisations that generally always create meeting places and activities that promote our physical and mental public health – from the extensive and professional work of sport on physical activity, dignity and inclusion, to the annual BBQ meetings of small associations. That is why we must give the voluntary economic conditions and not least maintain its freedom and arms. The simple is often the best. I would therefore like to strike a blow for the outdoors, the good Norwegian tradition of going for a walk in the nearby environment. In this context, the inter-municipal outdoor councils and the voluntary organisations of the outdoor community are doing a fantastic job, both with the physical organisation of open-air areas, which makes it easy to access and experience, and with the organisation of joint trips and experiences. It's fun to go for a walk, but it's often best to do it with others. Many without family and friends nearby have the opportunity to meet others through these organized activities, without costing anything, and in their community. Physical and mental health literally goes hand in hand here – on the move. As one of the health centers, it says in its motto, ‘Why sit in, when all hope is out?’ Against this background, the Right will support the Left's proposal No. Eight, nine, eleven. Our health cannot only be made the responsibility of the community. We also need to take personal responsibility. I think that the bigotry message, as it has been presented, balances these relationships. We must not get into the situation of undermining individual responsibility for our own health, while acknowledging that we can do a lot at the overall level. That is why perhaps some of the most important state contribution to public health is the regulation we have in the areas of alcohol and tobacco. Only in the area of alcohol are four key instruments that need to be relegated to the future. It is to have a level of alcohol that keeps consumption down. It's keeping the wine monopoly. It is to have regulation of sales times and outlets and gift times and venues, and it is to keep the advertising ban. In the area of tobacco, a high level of charges will be a significant instrument. Therefore, in its alternative state budgets, the Right also proposes a higher level of tobacco charges than has been proposed by the Red-Green Government. These are effective instruments at a national level that it is important to reinforce further.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e2755	Why is it that in Norway, where the standard of living is so good and the health status of the population is among the best in the world, we still need to put public health and increased efforts for prevention so high on the agenda? Ubuntu is a kind of common term for a lifestyle that conveys the idea that we become human through the meeting with others. “I’m me because you’re you because we’re something together – in a community,” they say in South Africa. Ubuntu is threatened on a tape that is universal and connects it to genuine human beings. It's a great word with an important reminder to all of us. Many people are concerned about – and know something about – how the creative forces in art and culture affect our health. At the Nord-Trøndelag University, research has been carried out on the participation of individuals and groups in cultural life and on the relationship with health. For many groups, the context is very clear. It is about pain relief, about improving the mental health of people, for example. to alleviate anxiety and to increase cognitive and linguistic skills. The Centre is organised as a foundation and operates largely through voluntary efforts. Here are a large number of activity groups in turn, with e.g. music, literature, local history and tour activities on the agenda – improving the health of participants as an important objective. User studies show that this works as intended. One of the participants at the centre said it this way: As a caring gardener stands a luxuriant flower, the NaKuHel-centre gave me exactly the nutrient-rich soil and a suitable climate that I got into and growing on. I'm concerned with the health-promoting perspective of public health work, what facilitates us to live happy, good, social and active lives. That perspective is central to the great story of the red-green government, from Soria Moria to today. That's what I would call ubuntu in the policy that's going to carry us in the future as well.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e3124	School is an important prevention arena. The curricula help children and young people to gain knowledge of how best to take care of one’s own health by eating healthy and being physically active. A good school health service is also important to prevent and help the many children and young people who are struggling with mental illness. Preventing damage can be a significant contribution to improving public health. Injury prevention forums are coordinators of 20 municipalities and Sogn and Fjordane counties, all of which are certified according to the World Health Organisation standard ‘Safe Communities’, or Safe communities, as it is called in Norwegian. Harstad became the first Norwegian certified municipality in 1994 and in the committee setting it is shown that Harstad, together with Os in Østerdalen, has been in the front for systematic damage prevention work, with good results. In Harstad, the health sister Solveig Rostøl Bakken and the surgeon and Professor Børge Ytterstad have been pioneers, and have put lasting traces behind them. Public health benefits are indisputable when scalding injuries in young children are virtually eradicated, and when treatment in the municipality and the elderly has been given the recipe for effective prevention of femoral fractures in the elderly. Cooperation across administrative and sectoral levels and good accident statistics has made it possible to put in place effective prevention measures. Safe local communities are based on a model and a methodology based on multidisciplinary cooperation and local efforts across the public and voluntary sectors. The benefits of greater efforts to prevent accidents are increased quality of life, better use of hospital capacity and a smart economy. Accidents are cause of death No. 1 in the 1–44 age group, not cancer or other diseases, which many people may think. Better identification of accident causes can therefore be the key to saved lives and more good years of life. A good record is therefore a good public health measure.

The Committee notes that these are the last two sections of the E18 Eastfold package and that there is local political support for the proposed package. In the context of the examination of the deployment and financing arrangements for the project E18 Krosby-Knapstad, it is also decided to extend the recovery period from 15 to 20 years for both E6 and E18. Rehabilitation and new construction of the 58 km long section is now to be completed with the last two sections of E18 in Eastfold. The majority of the Committee sees it as an important and necessary investment for both road safety and competitiveness. A different majority from what I have mentioned – the members of the Progress Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party – also believe that it is necessary for the E18 to be completed as a non-meeting road on the entire section of the Riksgren–Vinterbro by 2020. In order to ensure continuous expansion, the route from Retvet in Akershus to Winterbro in Akershus must also be started as originally planned for the first part of the planning period. Then I'm going to mention and point out some particular issues, seen from my party right. We will stress the importance of realizing precisely E18 between Winterbro and the Swedish border and are critical of this being done piece by piece and division. This has meant that the project has taken much longer and has become much more expensive than necessary. The Smålen newspaper on Saturday, 8 June this year says in the director of the newspaper: «E18 is never completed». The right share the view that E18 has become a textbook in short-term thinking and how to waste our tax revenue, both on government budgets and on tolls. The first municipal government treatment in Inner Eastfold of tolling E18 in Eastfold was already treated in 1989. We note that ‘forced expansion’ has taken 24 years today and will have taken a full 27 years in 2016, as the sections are expected to be completed. There is every reason to recall that former centre party leader Haga promised completion in the 2005 election campaign in 2009. E18 between Lundeby and Ørje has already become too narrow and does not meet today's requirements. A number of bridges and sections need to be expanded. The road must be converted from two-field to two-field with pass-by fields and middle parts, which is reported to be more expensive than if it had been built four-field road from the start of. The development of E18 through Inner Eastfold is an example of how not to plan and build for the future. The right considers that the four-field road on the entire Oslo–Riksgren route had been building for the future. Instead, we have built piece by piece and expensive. Right will have more road faster! Building overall, faster, continuously and with clear funding ensures more money. In this area, there is a need for modern solutions, which we absolutely need, and which the Right is calling for. We want the existing two-field and three-field sections of the E18 Oslo-Ørje/Riksgren to be upgraded to four-field roads and indicate that the Right has previously proposed to extend this as an overall public-private cooperation project and that the way would already have been completed with our solutions.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e3698	This case is about the state's ownership of Cermaq. I just want to start by saying that as late as May 14th this year, the Stortinget dealt with a matter of a state participation in a preferential distribution in Cermaq, and the acquisition of Copeinca, one of the largest fishmeal and fish oil producers in Peru. At the general meeting of the company on 21 May, 62.5 pst voted. for the transaction, which was then also agreed by the Storting. But there was not enough support to implement the financing of the Copeinca transaction. After that time, we have clearly seen that the activity towards Cermaq has increased. Therefore, it is absolutely necessary that the Stortinget also now perform the necessary rounds of state ownership of the company and handle the powers that the government now requests. In this way, this matter has been reviewed very quickly in the Committee, and I also thank the Committee for a thorough examination of the matter in the short time available. We already knew when we dealt with the previous case on 14 May that there was great interest in the company, including through the activity that Marine Harvest had shown to the company. In retrospect, we have registered, not least through the media, that even now there are several interested parties. We are also aware that there is now a specific offer on Cermaq ASA from Marine Harvest, but both Cermaq ASA's board of directors and the Ministry of Industry and Trade believe that the offer does not reflect the real values of the company. What is thus in progress is a never-ending race about the company's values. We got as late as today in a press release that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade is now initiating a process for the possible purchase of shares in Cermaq ASA, assuming that the Stortinge gives the necessary powers this evening. The important thing is to ensure and maintain the values of the company in a good way for both the state and other shareholders. In this sense, it is absolutely necessary for the State to exercise active ownership in the light of the current situation. An authorisation, as requested by the Government today by the Storting, considers that the majority of the Committee is necessary to manage the values in a good way and at the same time provide the State with sufficient tools to deal with the situation that has arisen. It is therefore important for the Storting to give an authorisation for the State to purchase shares in Cermaq ASA up to a holding of 65 p.m. and for the State to sell all of its shares at the same time. An extension of the sales authorisation will ensure that the State may also choose to allow Cermaq ASA to be merged with another operator, with the freedom to choose an appropriate settlement form. It is important to ensure that the values of Cermaq ASA are kept in the best possible way. The majority certainly believe that the authority they have set up to provide to the government is the right way to deal with the situation. Now I suppose the minority will explain their positions in an excellent way in relation to the matter, but I would like to stress that what is now, we should say, the Cermaq ASA race, testifies well that the space for action and the ability to exercise an active ownership might be limited by the proposal the minority chooses to vote for tonight. I'm actually a little surprised because the marine sector is an important strategic area for Norway, and in the years to come the farming industry can become part of a significant growth. The majority are therefore concerned – and I stress this – with the further assessments and strategic dispositions of a possible transaction, in fact assessing the possibilities of strategic ownership where head office functions, research and strategic functions have a main base in Norway – yes, with some state ownership in relation to it. I would like to strongly recommend the Stortinget to vote in favour of the suspension in its entirety as it exists, so that the State can be ensured through its ownership a sufficient space of action that can in turn contribute to a good industrial solution for the company and a fair price for the shares, possibly in Cermaq ASA. I would also like to believe that this is the last case of the Business Committee in Stortinget and would therefore like to thank both for the cooperation and everyone for a really good summer.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-20.ana.ud2501e3746	A unanimous increase today gives the government a sales authorisation to sell out of Cermaq ASA, the same authority that the Right has proposed on several previous occasions. Now the situation is something different than before. In the past, we have considered, in principle, that the State as a significant owner of Cermaq ASA with competitive surfaces against many wholly-owned private companies has a double role in the simultaneous administration, concession and supervisory functions. Our connection to the sale mandate this time is still based on the primary view that the double role is unfortunate, and we see that the situation can now be conducive to the government reducing or terminating its involvement. But we also see, of course, that sales can now contribute to an industrial solution in which Norwegian jobs are secured, developed and given an even better future. Such opportunities also exist and are already one of the elements in the game of the company currently operating through the Marine Harvest bid, a game in which the opposition has only limited transparency — and should have nothing more than that — and therefore not a background enough to form a qualified image of what is right to do. As we write in our notes, it's not impossible to make a purchase of 65 pst. can provide the company with flexibility for solutions, but we are strongly in doubt whether it is appropriate to use over 2 billion. kr of taxpayers' money for a project that is definitely presenting with a significant risk, especially when you do not know what one owns, with a purchase of 65 pst, except to be flexible. There will then be considerable uncertainty as to whether you can get out of the company again if you want to, and not least at what price. This is a cyclical industry, market prices fluctuate, trading conditions fluctuate, competition varies greatly, product quality, disease, environmental problems are some of the things we know from this area. We have no basis or information through the proposition that weakens these kind of considerations. And from the same point of view, i.e. the outside position, the development that is now coming, with new bids on parts of the company, seems to indicate that there may be a possibility that after a purchase, or after a sale, one can stand with 65 pst. in a Cermaq without the substantial part of the feed business, and then there is a company with a predominantly part of its activity abroad and a relatively modest share in Norwegian activity with Norwegian jobs. It gives, as we assess it today, without the knowledge that others have, an additional risk that the values you buy as a fully integrated company will not be realized when the sales object is a more amputated company. Indeed, both in the proposition and in the red-green notes, they have committed themselves to contributing to a good industrial solution, while at the same time obtaining a fair price, and not least to carrying out a transaction at the expense of shareholders, where the state's ownership can again be reduced. It's a long race with a lot of risk factors. We don't know how this is going to happen, but as I said, we are in considerable doubt that this very soluble sketch can be fulfilled. I would also like to stress that good industrial policy and good state ownership are not always just maximizing value, as stated often by the Council of State, at least in newspaper interviews, but also industrial solutions that take care of and secure Norwegian jobs. It's a recurring theme in the exercise of state ownership, and we don't have to go so far back in the red-green business history until we find examples of Norwegian employment considerations playing a far greater role than taking into account the values of the state or maximising them at all times. So this seems to be to a significant extent situational, and for those who don't know the situation, it's hard to go out on this race. That's why we're voting against the authority to buy up. It also has to do with what the representative of Aasland hangs at the end of his speech, that he is wondering about the opposition, which limits the possibility of participating in the race. That's right, we limit the possibilities of participating in a race when we only know where it starts, but not where it goes.
